<td width="50%" valign="top">He said: Then which of creation is strongest?<br>He (a.s.) said: The forbearing one.<br>He said: Then which of creation is most miserly?<br>He (a.s.) said: The one who takes wealth from other than its lawful source and then spends it [where it is not due].<br>He said: Then which of people is shrewdest?<br>He (a.s.) said: The one who discerns his right course from his error and inclines to his right course.<br>He said: Then who is the most forbearing of people?<br>He (a.s.) said: The one who does not become angry.<br>He said: Then which of people is firmest in judgement?<br>He (a.s.) said: The one whom people do not deceive about himself, and whom this world does not deceive with the longing it stirs.<br>He said: Then which of people is most foolish?<br>He (a.s.) said: The one deluded by this world while he sees how its conditions keep changing.<br>He said: Then which of people suffers the greatest regret?<br>He (a.s.) said: The one who is deprived of both this world and the hereafter; that is the manifest loss [Q 22:11].<br>He said: Then which of creation is blindest?<br>He (a.s.) said: The one who works for other than Allah while seeking, by his work, a reward from Allah, Mighty and Majestic.<br>He said: Then which contentment is best?<br>He (a.s.) said: That of the one content with what Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has given him.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> Continues narration [72]. The editor says the original has a blank at '[في غير حقّه]' and he filled it from al-Faqīh and al-Amālī. 'فأي الناس أكيس' is printed without a shadda on أي. 'من لم تغرّه الناس من نفسه' probably means that people's praise does not mislead him about his own worth. 'بتشوّقها' means by making itself longed for; the editor records al-Faqīh's reading بتشوّفها, 'by adorning itself'. 'ذلك هو الخسران المبين' matches the end of Q 22:11 (after خسر الدنيا والآخرة) and also occurs in Q 39:15. 'القنوع' is taken as contentment here (the word can also mean begging).</sub><br><sub>p. 333</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">قال: فأيّ المصائب أشدّ؟<br>قال (عليه السلام): المصيبة بالدين.<br>قال: فأيّ الأعمال أحبّ إلى الله عزّ وجلّ؟<br>قال (عليه السلام): انتظار الفرج.<br>قال: فأيّ الناس خيرٌ عند الله عزّ وجلّ؟<br>قال (عليه السلام): أخوفهم لله، وأعملهم بالتقوى، وأزهدهم في الدنيا.<br>قال: فأيّ الكلام أفضل عند الله عزّ وجلّ؟<br>قال (عليه السلام): كثرة ذكره، والتضرّع إليه بالدعاء.<br>قال: فأيّ القول أصدق؟<br>قال (عليه السلام): شهادة أن لا إله إلاّ الله.<br>قال: فأيّ الأعمال أعظم عند الله؟<br>قال (عليه السلام): التسليم والورع.<br>قال: فأيّ الناس أصدق؟<br>قال: من صدّق في المواطن.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">He said: Then which calamity is most severe?<br>He (a.s.) said: Calamity in one's religion.<br>He said: Then which deeds are most beloved to Allah, Mighty and Majestic?<br>He (a.s.) said: Awaiting relief (al-faraj).<br>He said: Then which of people is best in the sight of Allah, Mighty and Majestic?<br>He (a.s.) said: The one among them who fears Allah most, acts most by taqwa, and is most detached from this world.<br>He said: Then which speech is most excellent in the sight of Allah, Mighty and Majestic?<br>He (a.s.) said: Much remembrance of Him, and humbly beseeching Him in supplication.<br>He said: Then which saying is most truthful?<br>He (a.s.) said: The testimony that there is no god but Allah.<br>He said: Then which deeds are greatest in the sight of Allah?<br>He (a.s.) said: Submission (taslīm) and scrupulous piety (waraʿ).<br>He said: Then which of people is most truthful?<br>He said: The one who proves true in the decisive moments.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> Continues narration [72]. 'انتظار الفرج' is awaiting relief or deliverance. 'خيرٌ' is printed with tanwin. The editor notes that al-Amālī has 'أكرم' (most honoured) instead of 'أصدق' in the last question. The last answer has no honorific in the print. 'صدّق' is printed with a shadda. The familiar idiom صَدَقَ في المواطن means he stood firm and proved true in the battle-stations or critical situations (المواطن often means battlefields, cf. Q 9:25). Read with the shadda, it could instead mean 'who affirmed [the truth] in those situations'.</sub><br><sub>p. 334</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">ثمّ أقبل (عليه السلام) على الشيخ فقال: يا شيخ. إنّ الله عزّ وجلّ خلق خلقاً ضيّق الدنيا عليهم، نظر إليهم، فزهّدهم فيها و [في] حطامها، فرغبوا في دار السلام التي دعاهم إليها، وصبروا على ضيق المعيشة، وصبروا على المكروه، واشتاقوا إلى ما عند الله عزّ وجلّ من الكرامة، فبذلوا أنفسهم ابتغاء رضوان الله، وكانت خاتمة أعمالهم الشهادة، فلقوا الله عزّ وجلّ وهو عنهم راض، وعَلِموا أنّ الموت سبيل من مضى وسبيل من بقي، فتزوّدوا لآخرتهم غير الذهب والفضّة، ولبسوا الخشن، وصبروا على الطول، وخدموا الفضل، وأحبّوا في الله عزّ وجلّ وأبغضوا في الله عزّ وجلّ، أولئك المصابيح وأهل النعيم في الآخرة والسلام.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Then he (a.s.) turned to the old man and said: O old man. Allah, Mighty and Majestic, created a people for whom He made this world narrow. He looked upon them [with care] and made them renounce it and [renounce] its worthless debris. So they longed for the Abode of Peace to which He had invited them. They were patient with a narrow livelihood and patient with hardship, and they yearned for the honour that is with Allah, Mighty and Majestic. So they gave up their lives seeking the good pleasure of Allah, and the seal of their deeds was martyrdom, and they met Allah, Mighty and Majestic, while He was pleased with them. They knew that death is the path of those who have gone and the path of those who remain, so they took provision for their hereafter of something other than gold and silver. They wore coarse clothing, endured patiently through the long haul, served virtue, loved for the sake of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, and hated for the sake of Allah, Mighty and Majestic. Those are the lamps, and the people of bliss in the hereafter. And peace.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> Continues narration [72], running from p. 334 to p. 335. There is a period after 'يا شيخ' in the print. 'نظر إليهم' stands without a conjunction and is read as 'He looked upon them with care or favour'; the editor records al-Amālī's reading نظراً لهم, 'out of care for them'. '[في]' is the editor's addition from al-Amālī. 'حطام' is the debris, i.e. the worthless goods of this world. 'دار السلام التي دعاهم إليها' echoes Q 10:25 ('Allah invites to the Abode of Peace'). Printed 'وعَلِموا' is from al-Amālī; the original has واعلموا. 'وصبروا على الطول' is uncertain: الطول may mean 'the length [of their trial]', or الطَّوْل 'means / abundance', i.e. they were patient in the face of plenty. The editor notes al-Faqīh's reading البلوى (affliction) and al-Amālī's القوت (bare sustenance). 'وخدموا الفضل' is also uncertain: it is rendered literally as 'served virtue (al-faḍl)'. The editor notes that al-Faqīh and al-Amālī read وقدّموا الفضل, 'they sent ahead their surplus [as charity]', which makes better sense. 'والسلام' is a conventional closing: 'and peace', or 'that is all'.</sub><br><sub>p. 334</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">قال الشيخ: وأين أذهب وأدع الجنّة وأنا أراها وأرى أهلها معك يا أمير المؤمنين؟ جهّزوني بقوّة أقوى بها على عدوّك، فأعطاه أمير المؤمنين (عليه السلام) سلاحاً، وحمله على الخيل، وكان في الحرب بين يدي أمير المؤمنين (عليه السلام) يضرب قُدُماً، وأمير المؤمنين (عليه السلام) يعجب ممّا يصنع.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">The old man said: And where would I go, leaving Paradise behind, when I can see it, and see its people with you, O Amir al-Mu'minin? Equip me with a force by which I may be strong against your enemy. So Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.) gave him weapons and mounted him on a horse. In the battle he fought in front of Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.), striking as he pressed forward, while Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.) marvelled at what he was doing.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> Continues narration [72]. The editor notes al-Faqīh's reading 'جهّزني' (singular) for 'جهّزوني' and 'أتقوّى' for 'أقوى'. 'بقوّة' means with strength, i.e. arms and gear. 'حمله على الخيل' literally means 'mounted him on the horses', i.e. gave him a mount. 'يضرب قُدُماً' means striking while pressing boldly ahead without turning back.</sub><br><sub>p. 335</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">فلمّا اشتدّت الحرب أقبل بفرسه حتّى قتل رحمة الله عليه، وأتبعه رجل من أصحاب أمير المؤمنين (عليه السلام) فوجده صريعاً، ووجد دابّته ووجد سيفه في ذراعه.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">When the battle grew fierce, he advanced on his horse until he was killed, Allah's mercy be upon him. A man from the companions of Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.) followed after him and found him struck down, and he found his mount, and found his sword still in his arm.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> Continues narration [72]. The editor notes al-Faqīh's reading 'أقدم' (charged forward) for 'أقبل'. 'قتل' is unvocalized and is read as the passive قُتِل. 'ذراع' is the forearm, i.e. the sword was still gripped in his hand.</sub><br><sub>p. 335</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">فلمّا انقضت [الحرب] أتي أمير المؤمنين (عليه السلام) بدابّته وسلاحه، وصلّى [عليه] أمير المؤمنين (عليه السلام)، وقال: هذا والله السعيد حقّاً، فترحّموا على أخيكم.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">When [the battle] was over, Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.) was brought the man's mount and weapons. Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.) prayed [over him] and said: By Allah, this one is truly the fortunate one. So ask for mercy upon your brother.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> End of narration [72]. The bracketed words are the editor's: [الحرب] from al-Amālī, [عليه] from al-Faqīh and al-Amālī. 'أتي' is unvocalized and is read as the passive أُتِيَ, 'was brought'. 'صلّى عليه' is the funeral prayer. 'السعيد' means the fortunate or felicitous one, i.e. blessed in the hereafter. The editor's source note lists Man lā yaḥḍuruh al-faqīh 4:273–275/829, Amālī al-Ṣadūq 322, Maʿānī al-akhbār 197, and Biḥār al-anwār 77:376/1. The narration ends on p. 335; p. 336 begins a new heading and is outside this chunk.</sub><br><sub>p. 335</sub></td>
</tr>
</table>


## The Testament of Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.) to His Son Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥanafiyya

<p dir="rtl" lang="ar"><b>وصية أمير المؤمنين (عليه السلام) لابنه محمّد بن الحنفيّة</b></p>

<table>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٧٣] وقال أمير المؤمنين (عليه السلام) في وصيّته لابنه محمّد بن الحنفيّة: يا بنيّ، إيّاك والإتّكال على الأماني، فإنّها بضائع النّوكي، وتثبيط عن الآخرة، ومن خير حظّ المرء قرين صالح.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[73] Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.) said in his testament (waṣiyya) to his son Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥanafiyya: My son, beware of relying on wishful hopes, for they are the merchandise of fools and a hindrance from [striving for] the Hereafter. Among the best of a person's fortune is a righteous companion.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> al-nawkā = fools (plural of anwak), as the editor's footnote glosses it. No isnad: the narration is introduced directly with 'wa-qāla'. The whole testament (pp. 336-342) is one narration, no. 73; it is split here into its paragraphs.</sub><br><sub>p. 336</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">جالس أهل الخير تكن منهم، باين أهل الشرّ ومن يصدّك عن ذكر الله عزّوجلّ، وذكر الموت بالأباطيل المزخرفة، والأراجيف الملفّقة تبن منهم، ولا يغلبنّ عليك سوء الظّن بالله عزّ وجلّ، فإنّه لن يدع بينك وبين خليلك صلحاً.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Sit with the people of good and you will be one of them. Keep apart from the people of evil, and from whoever turns you away from the remembrance of Allah, Mighty and Exalted, and from the remembrance of death with embellished falsehoods and fabricated rumors, and you will be distinct from them. Do not let thinking ill of Allah, Mighty and Exalted, overcome you, for it will leave no peace between you and your close friend.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> I read 'bi-l-abāṭīl al-muzakhrafa wa-l-arājīf al-mulaffaqa' as the means by which such people turn one away. Another possible reading takes it as a further object of 'bāyin' (keep apart from falsehoods and rumors). 'Tabin' (jussive of bāna) = you will be separate/distinct. 'Khalīlik' could be a human friend, or it could hint at one's relationship with Allah.</sub><br><sub>p. 336</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">ذكّ بالأدب قلبك كما يذكّى النار بالحطب، فنعم العون الأدب للخيّرة التجارب لذوي اللّبّ، أُضمم آراء الرجال بعضها إلى بعض ثمّ اختر أقربها إلى الثواب وأبعدها من الارتياب.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Kindle your heart with adab (good conduct and refinement) as fire is kindled with firewood. Adab is an excellent help to [one's good] nature, [as are] experiences to those of understanding. Put men's opinions together, one with another, then choose the one nearest to reward and furthest from doubt.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> 'li-l-khayyira': the editor notes that this is the reading of the original MS, while al-Faqīh and al-Amālī read 'li-l-naḥīza' (natural disposition), so I translate it as [good] nature. The print has no conjunction or comma before 'al-tajārib', so the 'and experiences' link is implied. 'aqrabuhā ilā al-thawāb' (nearest to reward): al-Faqīh reads 'al-ṣawāb' (nearest to what is correct), which fits the context better.</sub><br><sub>p. 336</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">يا بنيّ، لا شرف أعلى من الإسلام، ولا كرم أغنى من التقوى، ولا معقل أحرز من الورع، ولا شفيع أنجح من التوبة، ولا لباس أجمل من العافية، ولا وقاية أمنع من السلامة، ولا كنز أقنع من القنوع، ولا مال أذهب للفاقة من الرضا بالقوت.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">My son, there is no honor higher than Islam, no nobility richer than taqwā (God-consciousness), no stronghold more secure than waraʿ (scrupulous piety), no intercessor more successful than repentance, no garment more beautiful than well-being, no protection more shielding than safety, no treasure more satisfying than contentment, and no wealth that does more to remove poverty than being content with [bare] sustenance.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> This paragraph runs from p. 336 onto p. 337. Under zoom, 'aghnā' (richer) and 'aqnaʿ min al-qunūʿ' (lit. 'more contenting than contentment') are both clearly printed.</sub><br><sub>p. 336</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">ومن اقتصر على بلغة الكفاف، فقد انتظم الراحة، وتبوّأ خفض الدعة، الحرص داع إلى التقحّم في الذنوب، ألق عنك واردات الهموم بعزائم الصبر، [عوّد نفسك الصبر فنعم الخلق الصبر،] وحملها على ما أصابك من أهوال الدنيا وهمومها.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Whoever limits himself to a bare sufficiency (bulghat al-kafāf) has secured ease and settled into comfortable repose. Greed is a summoner to plunging headlong into sins. Cast off the worries that descend on you through resolute patience. [Accustom your soul to patience, for patience is an excellent trait,] and make it bear whatever terrors and worries of this world befall you.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The bracketed clause is supplied by the editor from al-Faqīh. The editor notes that the original read 'wa-inna ḥamlahā' and that the feminine pronoun refers to the soul (nafs). I read 'ḥammilhā' as an imperative (make it bear). 'intaẓama al-rāḥa' is literally 'has strung together / gathered in ease'.</sub><br><sub>p. 337</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">فاز الفائزون [ونجا] الذين سبقت لهم من الله الحسنى، فإنّه جنّة من الفاقة، والجأ نفسك في الأمور كلّها إلى [الله] الواحد القهّار فإنّك تلجئها إلى كهف حصين، وحرز حريز، ومانع عزيز، واخلص المسألة لربّك فإنّ بيده الخير والشرّ، والإعطاء والمنع، والصلّة والحرمان.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">The winners have won, and those for whom the best [reward] from Allah has already been decreed have been saved. For it is a shield against poverty. In all matters, commit your soul to [Allah], the One, the All-Compelling, for you will be committing it to a secure cave, a well-guarded refuge, and a mighty defender. Make your petition to your Lord alone, for in His hand are good and evil, giving and withholding, bestowal and deprivation.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> 'alladhīna sabaqat lahum min Allāh al-ḥusnā' echoes Q 21:101 ('those for whom the best has gone before from Us'). '[wa-najā]' and '[Allāh]' are editorial insertions from al-Faqīh. The antecedent of 'fa-innahu junnatun min al-fāqa' (it is a shield against poverty) is unclear: it may be patience, from the previous paragraph, or contentment. 'al-ṣilla' is printed with a shadda; I take it as al-ṣila (gift, bestowal), the natural pair of ḥirmān.</sub><br><sub>p. 337</sub></td>
</tr>
</table>


### And he (a.s.) said in this testament:

<p dir="rtl" lang="ar"><b>وقال (عليه السلام) في هذه الوصيّة:</b></p>

<table>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">يا بنيّ، الرزق رزقان؛ رزق تطلبه ورزق يطلبك، فإن لم تأته أتاك، فلا تحمل همّ سنتك على همّ يومك، وكفاك كلّ يوم ما هو فيه، فإن تكن السنة من عمرك فإنّ الله عزّ وجلّ سيأتيك في كلّ غد بجديدة ما قسّم لك فإن لم تكن السنة من عمرك فما تصنع بغمّ وهمّ ما ليس لك.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">My son, rizq (provision) is of two kinds: provision that you seek, and provision that seeks you; if you do not come to it, it will come to you. So do not load the worry of your year onto the worry of your day; each day's own concerns are enough for you. If the year is [part] of your lifespan, then Allah, Mighty and Exalted, will bring you, with each new tomorrow, what He has apportioned for you. And if the year is not [part] of your lifespan, then what will you do with grief and worry over what is not yours?<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> 'bi-jadīdati mā qassama lak' is literally 'with the new [portion] of what He apportioned you'. al-Faqīh reads 'bi-jadīd'.</sub><br><sub>p. 337</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">واعلم أنّه لم يسبقك إلى رزقك طالب، ولن يغلبك عليه [غالب]، ولن يحتجب عنك ما قدّر لك، فكم رأيت من طالب متعب نفسه مقترٍ عليه رزقه، ومقتصد في الطلب قد ساعدته المقادير، وكلّ [مقرون] به الفناء، اليوم لك وأنت من بلوغ غد على غير يقين، ولربّ مستقبل يوماً ليس بمستدبره، ومغبوط في أوّل ليلة قام في آخرها بواكيه، فلا يغرنّك من الله طول حلول النعم، وإبطاء موارد النقم فإنّه لو خشي الفوت عاجل بالعقوبة قبل الموت.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Know that no seeker will reach your provision before you, no [rival] will overpower you for it, and what has been decreed for you will not be kept from you. How many a seeker have you seen wearing himself out, yet his provision is straitened for him, and [how many] a one moderate in seeking whom the decrees have assisted! Everyone has annihilation joined to him. Today is yours, but you are not certain of reaching tomorrow. Many a one who sets out on a day will not see its end, and many a one envied at the start of a night has his mourners rising [in lament] at its end. So do not be deceived by the long stay of Allah's blessings and the slowness of His punishments' arrival, for if He feared losing the chance, He would hasten the punishment before death.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> This paragraph runs from p. 337 onto p. 338. '[ghālib]' and '[maqrūn]' are the editor's, from al-Faqīh; the original had blanks. 'mustaqbil yawman laysa bi-mustadbirih' is literally 'one who faces a day who will not turn his back on it', i.e. he will not live to its end. 'law khashiya al-fawt': Allah does not hurry punishment because nothing escapes Him.</sub><br><sub>p. 337</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">يا بنيّ، إقبل من الحكماء مواعظهم، وتدبّر أحكامهم، وكن أخذ الناس بما تأمر به، وأكفّ الناس عمّا تنهي عنه، وأمر بالمعروف تكن من أهله، فإنّ استتمام الأمور عند الله تبارك وتعالى الأمر بالمعروف والنهي عن المنكر، وتفقّه في الدين فإنّ العلماء ورثة الأنبياء، أنّ الأنبياء لم يورثوا ديناراً ولا درهماً ولكنّهم ورّثوا العلم، فمن أخذ منه أخذ بحظّ وافر.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">My son, accept the admonitions of the wise and reflect on their judgments. Be the most observant of people in [doing] what you enjoin, and the most restrained of people from what you forbid. Enjoin what is right (al-maʿrūf) and you will be among its people, for the completion of affairs in the sight of Allah, Blessed and Exalted, is enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong. Seek deep understanding (tafaqquh) in the religion, for the scholars are the heirs of the prophets. The prophets did not bequeath a dinar or a dirham; rather, they bequeathed knowledge, so whoever takes from it has taken an abundant share.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> 'aḥkāmahum' could also mean 'their wise maxims'. 'akhdh al-nās' is printed unvocalized; I read it as ākhadh (the one who adheres most). 'anna al-anbiyāʾ' is printed without a conjunction; I read it as explanatory ('for the prophets...'). The editor notes that al-Faqīh reads 'al-fuqahāʾ' for 'al-ʿulamāʾ'.</sub><br><sub>p. 338</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">واعلم أنّ طالب العلم يستغفر له من في السّماوات والأرض حتّى الطير في جوّ السماء والحوت في البحر، وإنّ الملائكة لتضع أجنحتها لطالب العلم رضي به وفيه شرف الدنيا والفوز بالجنّة يوم القيامة، لأنّ الفقهاء هم الدعاة إلى الجنان، والأدلاّء على الله تبارك وتعالى.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Know that whoever is in the heavens and the earth seeks forgiveness for the seeker of knowledge, even the birds in the open sky and the fish in the sea, and the angels lower their wings for the seeker of knowledge out of pleasure with him. In it lie the honor of this world and the triumph of Paradise on the Day of Resurrection, because the fuqahāʾ (jurists, those learned in the religion) are the callers to the Gardens and the guides to Allah, Blessed and Exalted.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> 'riḍī bihi' (printed without tanwīn) I read as riḍan bihi (out of pleasure with him). Another reading attaches it to the next clause. 'fīhi' (in it) most likely refers to knowledge or the seeking of it.</sub><br><sub>p. 338</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">وأحسن إلى جميع الناس كما تحبّ أن يحسن إليك، وارض لهم ممّا ترضاه، واستقبح لنفسك ممّا تستقبحه من غيرك، أحسن مع جميع النّاس خلقك حتّى إذا غبت عنهم حنّوا إليك، وإذا متّ بكوا عليك وقالوا: إنّا لله وإنّا إليه راجعون، ولا تكن من الذّين يقال عند موته: الحمد لله ربّ العالمين.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Do good to all people as you would love good to be done to you. Be pleased for them with what you are pleased with, and find ugly in yourself what you find ugly in others. Make your character good with all people, so that when you are away from them they long for you, and when you die they weep over you and say, 'Indeed we belong to Allah, and to Him we shall return' [Q 2:156]. And do not be among those at whose death it is said, 'Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds' [cf. Q 1:2].<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> This paragraph runs from p. 338 onto p. 339. The editor gives al-Faqīh's readings: 'mā tarḍāhu li-nafsik' (what you are pleased with for yourself), 'min nafsika mā', and 'wa-ḥassin' for 'aḥsin'. 'mimmā' is partitive (of what). 'yuqālu ʿinda mawtih' uses a singular pronoun after the plural 'alladhīna'; I have smoothed it in the English.</sub><br><sub>p. 338</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">واعلم انّ رأس العقل بعد الإيمان بالله عزّوجلّ مداراة الناس، ولا خير فيمن لا يعاشر بالمعروف [مَن لابدّ مِن معاشرته حتى يجعل الله إلى الخلاص منه سبيلاً، فإنّي وجدت جميع ما يتعايش به الناس وبه يتعاشرون ملء مكيال، ثلثاه استحسان وثلثه تغافل،] وما خلق الله عزّ وجلّ شيئاً أحسن من الكلام ولا أقبح منه، بالكلام ابيضّت الوجوه، وبالكلام اسودّت الوجوه.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Know that the head of reason, after faith in Allah, Mighty and Exalted, is mudārāt al-nās (tactful, courteous dealing with people). There is no good in one who does not treat kindly [someone he cannot avoid associating with, until Allah makes a way for him to be free of that person. For I have found that everything people live and associate with one another by is [like] a full measure (milʾ mikyāl): two-thirds of it is regarding [things] favorably, and one-third is deliberately overlooking.] Allah, Mighty and Exalted, has created nothing more beautiful than speech, nor anything uglier. Through speech faces have become bright, and through speech faces have become dark.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The long bracketed passage is the editor's, from al-Faqīh. 'taghāful' is deliberately feigning unawareness of others' faults. The bright and dark faces echo Q 3:106.</sub><br><sub>p. 339</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">واعلم أنّ الكلام في وثاقك ما لم تتكلّم به فإذا تكلمت به صرت في وثاقه فاخزن لسانك كما تخزن ذهبك وورقك، فإنّ اللسان كلب عقور بأن أنت خليته عقر، وربّ كلمة سلبت نعمة، من سيّب عذاره قاده إلى [كلّ] كريهة وفضيحة، ثمّ لم يخلّص من وهده إلاّ على مقت من الله عزّ وجلّ، وذمّ من الناس.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Know that speech is in your bonds as long as you have not uttered it; once you utter it, you are in its bonds. So store up your tongue as you store up your gold and silver, for the tongue is a biting dog: if you let it loose, it bites. Many a word has stripped away a blessing. Whoever lets his bridle hang loose will be led by it to [every] detestable thing and disgrace, and then he is not rescued from its pit except [to end up] under the loathing of Allah, Mighty and Exalted, and the censure of people.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> 'bi-an anta khallaytahu' is clearly printed with bāʾ (checked under zoom); one would expect 'fa-in' (if). I translate it as 'if'. 'sayyaba ʿidhārahu' means he left his bridle-strap loose, i.e. let his tongue run unchecked (editor's gloss). '[kull]' is from al-Faqīh. 'wahdihi' (its pit or low ground): the editor notes that al-Faqīh reads 'dahrihi', which gives 'he does not get free for the rest of his life except...'. 'yukhallaṣ' is printed with a shadda.</sub><br><sub>p. 339</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">قد خاطر بنفسه من استغنى برأيه، ومن استقبل وجوه الآراء عرف مواقع الخطأ، من تورّط في الأمور غير ناظر في العواقب قد تعرّض مقطّعات النوائب، والتدبير قبل العوامل، يؤمنك من الندم، والعاقل من وعظه التجارب وفي التجارب علم مستأنف، وفي تقلب الأحوال عرف جواهر الرجال، الأيّام تهتك لك عن السرائر الكامنة.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Whoever is content with his own opinion alone has put himself at risk, and whoever considers the various sides of opinions knows where the mistakes lie. Whoever plunges into affairs without looking at the consequences has exposed himself to calamities that cut him down. Planning before the deeds keeps you safe from regret. The intelligent person is the one whom experiences admonish; in experiences there is fresh knowledge, and in the changing of circumstances the true natures (jawāhir, lit. 'essences, jewels') of men are known. The days tear [the veil] away from hidden secrets for you.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The editor gives al-Faqīh's readings 'li-mufẓiʿāt' (to appalling [calamities]) for 'muqaṭṭiʿāt' and 'al-ʿamal' (before action) for 'al-ʿawāmil'. 'ʿurifa' is printed unvocalized; I read it as passive (are known), while 'ʿarafa' (one knows) is also possible. 'waʿaẓahu' is masculine though its subject, al-tajārib, is plural.</sub><br><sub>p. 339</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">فافهم وصيّتي هذه ولا تذهبنّ عنك صفحاً، فإنّ خير القول ما نفع.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">So understand this testament of mine, and do not let it slip away from you disregarded, for the best speech is that which benefits.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> 'lā tadhhabanna ʿanka ṣafḥan' is literally 'let it not go from you by way of turning aside'. It could also be read as 'do not turn away from it'.</sub><br><sub>p. 340</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">اعلم يا بنيّ، إنّه لابدّ لك من حسن الإرتياد، وبلاغك من الزّاد ومع خفّة الظهر فلا تحمل على ظهرك فوق طاقتك فيكون عليك ثقيلاً في حشرك ونشرك في القيامة، فبئس الزاد إلى المعاد العدوان على العباد، واعلم أنّ أمامك مهالك ومهاوي [وجسوراً] وعقبة كؤداً لا محالة وأنت هابطها وإنّ مهبطها أمّا على جنّة أو على نار، فارتد لنفسك قبل نزولك إيّاها، فإذا وجدت من أهل الفاقة، من يحمل زادك إلى القيامة فيوافيك فيه غدا حيث تحتاج إليه فاغتنم وحمّله وأكثر من تزوّده وأنت قادر عليه فلعلك تطلبه فلا تجده، وإيّاك أن تثق لتحميل زادك من لا ورع له ولا أمانة فيكون مثلك [مثل] ظمآن أتى سرابا حتّى إذا جاءه لم يجده شيئاً فتبقى في يوم القيامة منقطعاً بك.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Know, my son, that you cannot do without seeking out [the way] well, and [taking] enough provision to reach [your goal] while keeping your back light. So do not load onto your back more than you can bear, lest it weigh on you at your gathering and your raising up on [the Day of] Resurrection. How evil a provision for the Return is aggression against [Allah's] servants! Know that ahead of you, inevitably, are places of ruin, abysses [and bridges], and a hard mountain pass that you will descend, and its descent leads either to a Garden or to a Fire. So seek out [a good place] for yourself before you alight there. If you find among the needy someone who will carry your provision to the Resurrection and hand it back to you there tomorrow, when you need it, seize the opportunity and load him with it, and give him plenty to carry while you are able, for perhaps you will look for him and not find him. Beware of trusting, to carry your provision, someone who has no scrupulousness and no trustworthiness, lest your likeness be [the likeness of] a thirsty man who came to a mirage until, when he reached it, he found it to be nothing [cf. Q 24:39], and you would be left stranded on the Day of Resurrection.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> '[wa-jusūran]' and '[mathal]' are the editor's, from al-Faqīh. 'wa-balāghika min al-zād wa-maʿa khiffat al-ẓahr': the syntax is loose, and I take 'balāghik' as a second object of 'lā budda laka min'. 'ʿaqaba kaʾūd' (printed كؤداً) is a steep, hard-to-climb mountain pass. 'fa-yuwāfīka fīhi' could mean 'there' (at the Resurrection) or 'with it'. The mirage image paraphrases Q 24:39.</sub><br><sub>p. 340</sub></td>
</tr>
</table>


### And he (a.s.) said in this testament:

<p dir="rtl" lang="ar"><b>وقال (عليه السلام) في هذه الوصيّة:</b></p>

<table>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">يا بنيّ، البغي سابق إلى الجبن، لن يهلك امرء عرف قدره، من خطر شهوته صان قدره، قيمة كلّ امرأ ما يحسنه، الاعتبار يفيدك الرشاد وأشرف الغنى ترك المنى، الحرص فقرٌ حاضر، المودّة قرابة مستفادة، صديقك أخوك لأبيك وأُمّك، وليس كلّ أخ لك لأبيك وأُمّك صديقك، لا تتّخذن عدوّ صديقك صديقاً فتعادي صديقك، كم من بعيد أقرب منك من قريب، وصول معدمٌ خيرٌ من مثر جاف.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">My son, transgression (baghy) runs ahead to cowardice. A man who knows his worth will never perish. Whoever [restrains] his desire preserves his worth. The value of every man is what he does well. Taking lessons [from events] brings you right guidance, and the noblest wealth is abandoning wishes. Greed is poverty already present. Affection is an acquired kinship. Your friend is your brother through father and mother, but not every brother of yours through father and mother is your friend. Do not take your friend's enemy as a friend, and so become an enemy to your friend. How many a distant one is nearer to you than a near one! A destitute man who keeps ties is better than a wealthy one who is aloof.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The editor notes that al-Faqīh reads 'al-ḥayn' (ruin) for 'al-jubn' (cowardice). 'man khaṭara shahwatahu' is clearly printed with khāʾ-ṭāʾ, but its meaning is uncertain: 'put at stake' fits poorly. I render it 'restrains' on the likely reading ḥaẓara (forbade). al-Faqīh reads 'ḥaṣṣana' (fortified [himself against]). 'imraʾ' and 'imraʾan' are printed as spelled. The editor took 'muthrin' (wealthy) from al-Faqīh; the original reads 'mubirr'.</sub><br><sub>p. 340</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">الموعظة كهف لمن وعاها، من منّ بمعروف أفسده، من أساء خلقه عذّب نفسه وكانت البغضة أولى به، ليس من العدل القضاء [بالظنّ] على الثقة.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Admonition is a refuge (lit. a cave) for whoever takes it to heart. Whoever reminds others of a favor he has done spoils it. Whoever has bad character torments himself, and hatred is most fitting for him. It is not justice to pass judgment against a trustworthy person [on suspicion].<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> '[bi-l-ẓann]' is the editor's, from al-Faqīh. 'wa-kānat al-bughḍa awlā bihi' could also mean 'hatred [of others] clings to him'.</sub><br><sub>p. 341</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">ما أقبح الأشر عند البطر والكائنة عند النائبة المغلظة والقسوة على الجاه، والخلاف على الصاحب، والحنث من ذي المرّوة، والغدر من السلطان.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">How ugly are: exultant insolence (al-ashar) in [a time of] elation (al-baṭar), al-kāʾina [likely for al-kaʾāba, despondency] in the face of a severe calamity, harshness [by one who holds] rank, opposing one's companion, oath-breaking by a man of honor (murūwa), and treachery by the ruler.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The editor gives al-Faqīh's readings 'al-ẓafar' (victory) for 'al-baṭar', which he says means the same, and 'al-kaʾāba' (despondency) for 'al-kāʾina'. As printed, al-kāʾina ('what comes about, an event') makes little sense, so I follow the context. 'al-mughliẓa' (severe): al-Faqīh reads 'al-muʿḍila'. 'al-qaswa ʿalā al-jāh' could mean 'harshness on account of rank'; al-Faqīh reads 'al-jār' (harshness toward the neighbor).</sub><br><sub>p. 341</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">كفر النعمة موق، ومجالسة الأحمق شوم، اعرف الحقّ لمن عرضه لك شريفاً كان أو وضيعاً.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Ingratitude for a blessing is foolishness (mūq). Keeping company with a fool is ill-omened. Acknowledge the truth when it is presented to you by anyone, whether noble or lowly.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The editor glosses 'mūq' as ingratitude arising from stupidity. 'shūm' is printed without hamza (for shuʾm). 'iʿrif al-ḥaqq li-man ʿaraḍahu lak' could also mean 'recognize the right of whoever sets it before you'.</sub><br><sub>p. 341</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">من ترك القصد جار، من تعدّى الحقّ ضاق مذهبه، كم من دنف قد نجا، وصحيح قد هوى، قد يكون اليأس إدراكاً والطمع هلاكاً، استعتب من رجوت عتابه، لا يثبينّ [؟] من أمر على غدر، الغدر شرّ لباس المرء المسلم، من غدر ما أخلق أن لا يوفي له، الفساد يبير الكثير والاقتصاد [ينمي اليسير].</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Whoever abandons the middle course strays. Whoever oversteps the truth finds his way narrowed. How many a gravely ill person has survived, and how many a healthy one has perished! Despair can be attainment, and greed ruin. Seek reconciliation with the one whose reproach you hope for. [Never pass the night resolved] on treachery in any matter. Treachery is the worst garment for a Muslim man. Whoever betrays: how fitting it is that faith not be kept with him! Corruption destroys much, and moderation [makes a little grow].<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> MOST UNCERTAIN: 'lā yathbīnna' is printed thus (checked under zoom) and is not a recognizable form. The editor notes that al-Faqīh reads 'tabayyan'. I conjecture 'lā tabītanna' (never pass the night) and read 'amr' as possibly 'imriʾ' (a person); both are tentative. 'istaʿtib man rajawta ʿitābahu' is also unclear: istiʿtāb is seeking reconciliation or goodwill. It may mean 'seek to win back those whose reproach you still hope for, i.e. who still care enough to reproach you'. The editor glosses 'danif' as one afflicted by a chronic illness and 'yubīru' as 'destroys'. '[yunmī al-yasīr]' is the editor's, from al-Faqīh.</sub><br><sub>p. 341</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">من الكرم، الوقار بالرحم، من كرم ساد، ومن تفهّم ازداد، [أمحض] أخاك النصيحة وساعده على كلّ حال ما لم يحملك على معصية الله عزّ وجلّ، زل معه حيث زال، لا تصرم أخاك على ارتياب ولا تقطعه دون استعتاب لعل له عذر وأنت تلوم، أقبل من متنصّل عذره فتنالك الشفاعة، وأكرم الذين بهم تصول، وازدد لهم على طول الصحّة برّاً وإكراماً وتبجيلاً وتعظيماً فليس جزاء من عظّم شأنك أن تضيّع من قدره، ولا بجزاء من سترك أن تسوءه.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Part of nobility is reverence toward kin. Whoever is noble becomes a leader, and whoever seeks understanding increases [in it]. Give your brother sincere counsel, and support him in every circumstance as long as he does not lead you into disobeying Allah, Mighty and Exalted; move with him wherever he moves. Do not break with your brother over a suspicion, and do not cut him off without first seeking reconciliation; perhaps he has an excuse while you are blaming [him]. Accept the excuse of one who disowns [his fault], and intercession will reach you. Honor those through whom you gain your strength, and increase toward them, through long [years of] well-being, in kindness, honor, reverence, and esteem, for it is no repayment to one who has raised your standing that you diminish his worth, nor any repayment to one who has covered [your faults] that you do him harm.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> This paragraph runs from p. 341 onto p. 342. The editor notes that al-Faqīh reads 'min al-karam al-wafāʾ bi-l-dhimam' (part of nobility is keeping one's pledges) against the original's 'al-waqār bi-l-raḥim', whose meaning is strained. '[amḥiḍ]' is from al-Faqīh. 'laʿalla lahu ʿudhr' is printed without the accusative ending. 'bihim taṣūlu' (by whom you attack or prevail); al-Faqīh reads 'naṣarak' (who helped you). 'ʿalā ṭūl al-ṣiḥḥa' (long well-being) may be a corruption of al-ṣuḥba (long companionship). 'satarak' (covered for you); al-Faqīh reads 'sarrak' (pleased you).</sub><br><sub>p. 341</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">أكثر البرّ ما استطعت بجليسك فإنّك إذا شئت رأيت رشده، من كساه الحياء ثوبه اختفى عن العيون عيبه، من تحرّى القصد خفت عليه المؤن، من لم يعط نفسه شهواتها أصاب رشده، مع كل شدّة رخاء، ومع كلّ أكلة غُصص.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">Do as much kindness as you can to the one who sits with you, for whenever you wish, you will see his right-mindedness. Whoever is clothed by modesty in its garment has his faults hidden from people's eyes. Whoever aims for moderation finds his burdens (muʾan, costs and provisioning) lightened. Whoever does not give his soul its desires attains right guidance. With every hardship there is ease, and with every mouthful there are chokings.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> 'fa-innaka idhā shiʾta raʾayta rushdahu' is unclear. It may mean that a companion treated kindly will show you sound conduct whenever you seek it.</sub><br><sub>p. 342</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">لا تنال نعمة إلاّ بعد أذى، لِنْ لمن غاضك تظفر بطلبتك، ساعات الهموم ساعات الكفّارات والساعات تنفد عمرك ولا خير في لذّة من بعدها النّار، وما خير بخير بعدها النار، وما شرّ بشرّ بعده الجنة، كلّ نعيم دون الجنّة محقور، وكلّ بلاء دون النّار عافية، لا تضيّعنّ حقّ أخيك اتّكالاً على ما بينك وبينه، فإنّه ليس لك بأخ من أضعت حقّه، لا يكوننّ أخوك على قطيعتك أقوى منك على صلة، ولا على الإساءة إليك أقوى منك على الإحسان إليه.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">No blessing is attained except after hardship. Be gentle with whoever angers you, and you will achieve what you seek. The hours of worry are hours of expiation, and the hours use up your life. There is no good in a pleasure that is followed by the Fire. No good is truly good if the Fire comes after it, and no evil is truly evil if Paradise comes after it. Every bliss short of Paradise is contemptible, and every affliction short of the Fire is well-being. Never neglect your brother's right, relying on what is between you and him, for someone whose right you have neglected is no brother to you. Let your brother never be stronger in cutting you off than you are in keeping ties, nor stronger in wronging you than you are in doing good to him.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> 'ghāḍaka' is clearly printed with ḍād (checked under zoom); I take it as ghāẓaka (angered you). 'aḍaʿta' (neglected) is the editor's correction; the original and some sources read 'aḍʿafa', which he calls a misreading. 'ʿalā ṣila' is printed without a pronoun (on keeping ties).</sub><br><sub>p. 342</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">يا بُنيّ، إذا قويت فاقو على طاعة الله عزّ وجلّ، وإذا ضعفت فاضعف عن معصية الله عزّ وجلّ، وإن استطعت أن لا تملّك المرأة من أمرها ما جاوز نفسها فافعل، فإنّه أدوم لجمالها، وأرخى لبالها، وأحسن لحالها، فإنّ المرأة ريحانة وليست بقهرمانة، فدارها على كلّ حال، وأحسن الصحبة لها فيصفو عيشك، واحتمل القضاء بالرضا، وإن أحببت أن تجمع خير الدنيا والآخرة فاقطع طمعك ممّا في أيدي النّاس، والسلام عليك ورحمة الله وبركاته.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">My son, when you are strong, be strong in obeying Allah, Mighty and Exalted, and when you are weak, be weak in disobeying Allah, Mighty and Exalted. If you can avoid giving a woman control over matters beyond her own self, do so, for that better preserves her beauty, gives her more peace of mind, and is better for her condition. For a woman is a fragrant flower (rayḥāna), not a steward (qahramāna, manager of the household's affairs). So treat her with gentle tact in every circumstance and keep good company with her, and your life will be serene. Bear [Allah's] decree with contentment. If you wish to gather the good of this world and the Hereafter, cut off your longing for what is in people's hands. Peace be upon you, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> 'mā jāwaza nafsahā' is literally 'what goes beyond her self', i.e. affairs beyond her own person.</sub><br><sub>p. 342</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">هذا آخر وصيّة محمّد بن الحنفية.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">This is the end of the testament of Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥanafiyya.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> That is, the testament addressed to Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥanafiyya. The editor's footnote gives the parallel as Man lā yaḥḍuruhu al-faqīh 4:275-280/830 and Biḥār al-anwār 73:160/7 (as printed). This closes the testament; p. 343 starts a new section (sayings of al-Ṣādiq, a.s.), which belongs to the next chunk.</sub><br><sub>p. 342</sub></td>
</tr>
</table>


## Admonitions and Wise Sayings of Imam al-Ṣādiq (a.s.)

<p dir="rtl" lang="ar"><b>مواعظ وحكم للإمام الصادق (عليه السلام)</b></p>

<table>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٧٤] وروى محمّد بن أبي عمير، عن أبان بن عثمان وهشام بن سالم ومحمّد بن حمران، عن الصادق (عليه السلام) قال: عجبت لمن فزع من أربع كيف لا يفزع إلى أربع؛ عجبت لمن خاف كيف لا يفزع إلى قوله عزّ وجلّ: ﴿حَسْبُنَا اللَّهُ وَنِعْمَ الْوَكِيلُ﴾، فإنّي سمعت الله عزّ وجلّ يقول بعقبها: ﴿فَانْقَلَبُوا بِنِعْمَةٍ مِنَ اللَّهِ وَفَضْلٍ لَمْ يَمْسَسْهُمْ سُوءٌ﴾. وعجبت لمن اغتمّ كيف لا يفزع إلى قوله تعالى: ﴿لاَ إِلَهَ إِلاَّ أَنْتَ سُبْحَانَكَ إِنِّي كُنْتُ مِنَ الظَّالِمِينَ﴾، فإنّي سمعت الله عزّ وجلّ يقول بعقبها: ﴿فَاسْتَجَبْنَا لَهُ وَنَجَّيْنَاهُ مِنَ الْغَمِّ وَكَذَلِكَ نُنْجِي الْمُؤْمِنِينَ﴾. وعجبت لمن مكر به كيف لا يفزع إلى قوله عزّ وجلّ: ﴿وَأُفَوِّضُ أَمْرِي إِلَى اللَّهِ إِنَّ اللَّهَ بَصِيرٌ بِالْعِبَادِ﴾، فإنّي سمعتُ الله عزّ وجلّ يقول بعقبها: ﴿فَوَقَاهُ اللَّهُ سَيِّئَاتِ مَا مَكَرُوا﴾. وعجبت لمن أراد الدنيا وزينتها كيف لا يفزع إلى قوله تعالى: ﴿مَا شَاءَ اللَّهُ لاَ قُوَّةَ إِلاَّ بِاللَّهِ﴾ فإني سمعت الله عزّ وجلّ يقول بعقبها: ﴿إِنْ تَرَنِ أَنَا أَقَلَّ مِنْكَ مَالاً وَوَلَدًا فَعَسَى رَبِّي أَنْ يُؤْتِيَنِي خَيْرًا مِنْ جَنَّتِكَ﴾ الآية، وعسى موجبة.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[74] Muḥammad ibn Abī ʿUmayr narrated from Abān ibn ʿUthmān, Hishām ibn Sālim and Muḥammad ibn Ḥumrān, from al-Ṣādiq (a.s.), who said: I marvel at one who is alarmed by four things: how does he not turn for refuge to four things? I marvel at one who is afraid: how does he not turn for refuge to His words, Mighty and Majestic: “Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best Guardian” [Q 3:173]? For I have heard Allah, Mighty and Majestic, say right after it: “So they returned with blessing from Allah and bounty; no harm touched them” [Q 3:174]. And I marvel at one who is grieved: how does he not turn for refuge to His words, Exalted: “There is no god but You; glory be to You! Indeed, I have been among the wrongdoers” [Q 21:87]? For I have heard Allah, Mighty and Majestic, say right after it: “So We answered him and rescued him from grief; and thus do We rescue the believers” [Q 21:88]. And I marvel at one against whom a plot is laid: how does he not turn for refuge to His words, Mighty and Majestic: “And I entrust my affair to Allah; indeed Allah sees His servants” [Q 40:44]? For I have heard Allah, Mighty and Majestic, say right after it: “So Allah protected him from the evils of what they plotted” [Q 40:45]. And I marvel at one who desires this world and its adornment: how does he not turn for refuge to His words, Exalted: “What Allah wills [comes to pass]; there is no power except through Allah” [Q 18:39]? For I have heard Allah, Mighty and Majestic, say right after it: “If you see me as less than you in wealth and children, then perhaps my Lord will give me something better than your garden” [Q 18:39–40], to the end of the verse. And [Allah’s] “perhaps” is binding.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The narration runs from p.343 onto the top of p.344. The saying plays on two senses of فزع: فزع من ‘to be alarmed by’ and فزع إلى ‘to run to for refuge’. بعقبها = ‘immediately after it’. The editor’s footnotes give the verse references (Āl ʿImrān 3:173–174, al-Anbiyāʾ 21:87–88, Ghāfir 40:44–45, al-Kahf 18:39–40), which match my own identification. وعسى موجبة: when ʿasā (‘perhaps’) comes from Allah it amounts to a firm promise; ‘necessitating’ or ‘a certainty’ would also work. The Quranic text follows the printed vowels as closely as the scan allows. Small Quranic-script marks such as the dagger alif may not be reproduced exactly.</sub><br><sub>p. 343</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٧٥] وروى محمّد بن زياد الأزديّ، عن أبان بن عثمان الأحمر، عن الصادق جعفر بن محمّد (عليهما السلام) أنّه جاء إليه فقال له: بأبي أنت وأُمّي يا بن رسول الله علّمني موعظة. فقال له: إن كان الله تبارك وتعالى قد تكفّل بالرّزق، فاهتمامك لماذا؟ وإن كان الرزق مقسوماً، فالحرص لماذا؟ وإن كان الحساب حقّاً، فالفرح لماذا؟ [وإن كان الخلف من الله عزّ وجلّ حقّاً فالبخل لماذا؟ وإن كانت العقوبة من الله عزّ وجلّ النار فالمعصية لماذا؟ وإن كان الموت حقّاً فالفرح لماذا؟]. وإن كان العرض على الله عزّ وجلّ حقّاً، فالمكر لماذا؟ وإن كان الشيطان عدوّاً، فالغفلة لماذا؟ وإن كان الممرّ على الصّراط حقّاً، فالعجب لماذا؟ وإن كان كلّ شي بقضاء الله وقدره، فالحزن لماذا؟ وإن كانت الدنيا فانية، فالطمأنينة لماذا؟</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[75] Muḥammad ibn Ziyād al-Azdī narrated from Abān ibn ʿUthmān al-Aḥmar, from al-Ṣādiq Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad (a.s.): [Abān] came to him and said to him, “May my father and mother be your ransom, O son of the Messenger of Allah, teach me an admonition.” He said to him: “If Allah, Blessed and Exalted, has guaranteed sustenance, then why your anxiety? If sustenance is apportioned, then why greed? If the reckoning is real, then why exultation? [If compensation from Allah, Mighty and Majestic, is real, then why miserliness? If the punishment from Allah, Mighty and Majestic, is the Fire, then why disobedience? If death is real, then why exultation?] If being presented before Allah, Mighty and Majestic, is real, then why scheming? If Satan is an enemy, then why heedlessness? If the crossing over the Ṣirāṭ (the bridge over Hell) is real, then why self-admiration? If everything happens by Allah’s decree and measure, then why grief? If this world is perishing, then why feel settled in it?”<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> This narration runs from p.344 to the top of p.345. It is not stated who ‘came to him’ (أنّه جاء إليه). Grammatically it is probably Abān, the last narrator named, though it could be an unnamed man. The honorific after جعفر بن محمّد is a small ligature that I read as (عليهما السلام); it might be (عليه السلام). According to the editor’s footnotes: the first فالفرح (after الحساب) reads فالجمع (‘then why hoard?’) in al-Faqīh; الخلف with fatḥa on the khāʾ means ‘compensation’, i.e., Allah’s replacement in this world and the next for what is spent; the bracketed lines are supplied from al-Faqīh; and al-Faqīh reads بقضاء من الله. العجب is rendered as ʿujb (self-admiration), which fits the Ṣirāṭ context better than ʿajab (wonder). شي is printed without a final hamza. فالطمأنينة: ‘then why feel settled/secure [in it]?’</sub><br><sub>p. 344</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٧٦] وقال (عليه السلام): إنّي لأرحم ثلاثة وحقّ لهم أن يرحموا: عزيز أصابته مذلّة بعد العزّ، وغنيّ أصابته حاجة بعد الغنى، وعالم يستخفّ به أهله والجهلة.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[76] And he (a.s.) said: I truly have pity for three, and they deserve to be pitied: a person of honour who has been struck by humiliation after honour; a rich person who has been struck by need after wealth; and a scholar who is belittled by his own people and by the ignorant.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The speaker is presumably still al-Ṣādiq (a.s.), from the context of the section. يرحموا is taken as passive (yurḥamū). أهله could mean ‘his family’ or ‘his own people/community’. Another reading is ‘by the people of his [scholarly] field’, but that is less likely here.</sub><br><sub>p. 345</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٧٧] وقال (عليه السلام): خمس هنّ كما أقول: ليس لبخيل راحة، ولا لحسود لذّة، ولا لمملوك وفاء، ولا لكذّاب مروّة، ولا يسود سفيه.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[77] And he (a.s.) said: There are five things that are just as I say: the miser has no rest; the envious has no pleasure; the slave has no loyalty; the liar has no muruwwa (manly virtue and honour); and the foolish never become leaders.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> لمملوك literally means ‘one who is owned’, i.e., a slave. The sense is presumably that someone not in control of himself cannot be relied on to keep faith. مروّة (muruwwa) covers decency, integrity and self-respect.</sub><br><sub>p. 345</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٧٨] قال رسول الله (صلى الله عليه وآله): إنّكم لن تسعوا الناس بأموالكم، فسعوهم بأخلاقكم.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[78] The Messenger of Allah (s.a.w.) said: You will never be able to reach all people with your wealth, so reach them with your good character.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> This is given without an isnad. وسع literally means ‘to have room for / encompass’: your wealth cannot stretch to cover everyone, but your character can.</sub><br><sub>p. 345</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٧٩] وروى يونس بن ظبيان، عن الصادق جعفر بن محمّد (عليهما السلام) أنه قال: الاشتهار بالعبادة ريبة، إنّ أبي حدّثني عن أبيه، عن جدّه (عليهم السلام) انّ رسول الله (صلى الله عليه وآله) قال: أعبد النّاس من أقام الفرائض، وأسخى الناس من أدّى زكاة ماله، وأزهد النّاس من اجتنب الحرام، وأتقي الناس من قال الحقّ فيما له وعليه، وأعدل الناس من رضي للناس ما يرضى لنفسه وكره له ما يكره لنفسه، وأكيس الناس من كان أشدّ ذكراً للموت، وأغبط الناس من كان تحت التراب قد أمن العقاب ويرجو الثواب، وأغفل الناس من لم يتّعظ بتغيّر الدنيا من حال إلى حال، وأعظم الناس في الدنيا خطراً من لم يجعل للدنيا عنده خطراً، وأعلم الناس من جمع علم الناس إلى علمه، وأشجع الناس من غلب هواه، وأكثر الناس قيمة أكثرهم علماً، وأقل الناس قيمة أقلّهم علماً، وأقل الناس لذّة الحسود، وأقل الناس راحة البخيل، وأبخل الناس من بخل بما افترض الله عليه، وأولى الناس بالحقّ أعلمهم به، وأقلّ الناس حرمة الفاسق، وأقلّ الناس وفاء المملوك، وأقلّ الناس صديقاً الملك، وأفقر الناس الطامع، وأغنى الناس من لم يكن للحرص أسيراً. وأفضل الناس إيماناً أحسنهم خلقاً، وأكرم الناس أتقاهم، وأعظم الناس قدراً من ترك ما لا يعنيه، وأورع الناس من ترك المراء وإن كان محقّاً، وأقلّ الناس مروّة من كان كاذباً، وأشقى الناس المملوك، وأمقت الناس المتكبّر وأشدّ الناس اجتهاداً من ترك الذنوب، وأحكم الناس من فرّ من جهّال الناس، [وأسعد الناس من خالط كرام الناس]، وأعقل الناس أشدّهم مداراة للناس، وأولى الناس بالتهمة من جالس أهل التهمة. وأعتى الناس من قتل غير قاتله أو ضرب غير ضاربه، وأولى الناس بالعفو أقدرهم على العقوبة، وأحقّ الناس بالذّنب السفيه المغتاب، وأذلّ الناس من أهان الناس، وأحزم الناس أكظمهم للغيظ، وأصلح الناس أصلحهم للناس، وخير الناس من انتفع به الناس.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[79] Yūnus ibn Ẓabyān narrated from al-Ṣādiq Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad (a.s.) that he said: Becoming famous for worship is a cause for suspicion. My father told me, from his father, from his grandfather (a.s.), that the Messenger of Allah (s.a.w.) said: The most devoted of people in worship is the one who performs the obligatory duties. The most generous of people is the one who pays the zakat (obligatory alms) on his wealth. The most detached of people from the world is the one who avoids the forbidden. The most God-wary of people is the one who speaks the truth, whether it is in his favour or against him. The most just of people is the one who is pleased for people with what he is pleased with for himself, and dislikes for each of them what he dislikes for himself. The shrewdest of people is the one who remembers death most intensely. The most enviable of people is the one beneath the soil who has become safe from punishment and hopes for reward. The most heedless of people is the one who takes no lesson from the world’s changing from one state to another. The greatest of people in worth in this world is the one who gives this world no worth in his own eyes. The most knowledgeable of people is the one who adds other people’s knowledge to his own. The bravest of people is the one who overcomes his own desires. The people of greatest value are those with the most knowledge, and the people of least value are those with the least knowledge. The person with the least pleasure is the envious. The person with the least rest is the miser. The most miserly of people is the one who is miserly with what Allah has made obligatory on him. The person with the most right to the truth is the one who knows it best. The person least owed respect is the fāsiq (open transgressor). The person with the least loyalty is the slave. The person with the fewest friends is the king. The poorest of people is the covetous, and the richest of people is the one who is not a captive of greed. The best of people in faith are those best in character. The most honoured of people is the most God-wary of them. The greatest of people in standing is the one who leaves what does not concern him. The most scrupulous of people is the one who gives up quarrelsome argument even when he is in the right. The person with the least muruwwa (manly virtue) is the one who lies. The most wretched of people is the slave. The most loathed of people is the arrogant. The person who strives hardest is the one who abandons sins. The wisest of people is the one who flees from ignorant people. [The happiest of people is the one who mixes with noble people.] The most intelligent of people is the one who is most tactful with people. The person most deserving of suspicion is the one who sits with people under suspicion. The most tyrannical of people is the one who kills someone other than his killer or strikes someone other than the one who struck him. The person most deserving to pardon is the one most able to punish. The person most deserving of the charge of sin is the foolish backbiter. The most abased of people is the one who humiliates people. The most prudent of people is the one best at restraining his anger. The most righteous of people is the one who does the most good for people. And the best of people is the one from whom people benefit.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> This narration runs from p.345 through p.346 to the first line of p.347. Several readings are uncertain. (1) وأتقي is printed with a dotted final yāʾ, presumably for وأتقى (the translation is the same). انّ before رسول الله is printed without a hamza, and أنه قال without shadda. (2) The honorifics after جعفر بن محمّد and after جدّه are small ligatures; I read them as (عليهما السلام) and (عليهم السلام) respectively, but either could be the other form. (3) The editor’s footnotes say that وأورع reads وأوزع in the original, which he treats as a scribal error corrected from al-Faqīh; that for أشقى الناس المملوك al-Faqīh has الملوك (‘the most wretched of people are kings’); and that the bracketed clause about the happiest of people is supplied from al-Faqīh. Other points: كره له (singular) is taken as ‘for [each] person’. أكيس = most astute/intelligent in the religious sense. أغبط means most enviable in the sense of ghibṭa, i.e., worthy to be emulated, not resented. خطراً = worth/importance. أولى الناس بالحقّ could also mean ‘most entitled to authority or right’. قتل غير قاتله refers to taking revenge on someone other than the actual offender, e.g., killing someone other than his kinsman’s killer. أحقّ الناس بالذنب could also mean ‘most liable to blame’. مداراة = courteous forbearance or tact toward people.</sub><br><sub>p. 345</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٨٠] ومرّ أمير المؤمنين (عليه السلام) برجل يتكلّم بفضول الكلام، فوقف عليه ثمّ قال: يا هذا إنّك تملي على حافظيك كتاباً إلى ربّك، فتكلّم بما يعنيك، ودع ما لا يعنيك.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[80] Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.) passed by a man who was engaged in idle, superfluous talk. He stopped by him and said: “O you! You are dictating to your two guardian [angels] a letter to your Lord, so speak about what concerns you and leave what does not concern you.”<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> حافظيك: dual, i.e., the two recording angels. فضول الكلام: excess or needless speech.</sub><br><sub>p. 347</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٨١] وقال (عليه السلام): لا يزال الرجل [المسلم] يكتب محسناً ما دام ساكتاً، فإذا تكلّم كتب محسناً أو مسيئاً.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[81] And he (a.s.) said: A [Muslim] man continues to be recorded as a doer of good as long as he stays silent. Once he speaks, he is recorded either as a doer of good or as a doer of evil.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> From the sequence, ‘he’ is presumably Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.), though the book does not say so. يكتب is read as passive (yuktabu). The editor supplies [المسلم] from al-Faqīh, and notes that al-Khiṣāl has المؤمن in place of الرجل.</sub><br><sub>p. 347</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٨٢] وقال الصادق (عليه السلام): الصمت كنز وافر، زين الحليم وستر الجاهل.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[82] Al-Ṣādiq (a.s.) said: Silence is an abundant treasure: an adornment for the forbearing and a covering for the ignorant.<br><sub>p. 347</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٨٣] وقال (عليه السلام): كلام في حقّ خير من سكوت على باطل.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[83] And he (a.s.) said: Speaking in the cause of truth is better than keeping silent about falsehood.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> According to the editor’s footnote, the original has في before باطل (‘silence in falsehood’), and he emended it to على. The speaker is presumably al-Ṣādiq (a.s.), who is named in the previous item.</sub><br><sub>p. 347</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٨٤] وروى إسماعيل بن مسلم، عن الصادق جعفر بن محمّد، عن أبيه، عن آبائه (عليهم السلام) قال: قال أمير المؤمنين (عليه السلام): كانت الفقهاء والحكماء إذا كاتب بعضهم بعضاً كتبوا بثلاث ليس معهنّ رابعة: من كانت الآخرة همّه كفاه الله همّه من الدنيا، ومن أصلح سريرته أصلح الله علانيته، ومن أصلح فيما بينه وبين الله أصلح الله فيما بينه وبين الناس.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[84] Ismāʿīl ibn Muslim narrated from al-Ṣādiq Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad, from his father, from his forefathers (a.s.), who said: Amir al-Mu'minin (a.s.) said: When the jurists and the wise wrote to one another, they would write three things with no fourth alongside them: whoever makes the Hereafter his concern, Allah will relieve him of his worldly concerns; whoever sets right his inner self, Allah will set right his outward life; and whoever sets right what is between him and Allah, Allah will set right what is between him and people.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> This narration begins on p.347 and ends on p.348. After it the rest of p.348 is blank, so the chunk ends at a natural break and p.349 was not needed. There is no honorific after جعفر بن محمّد in this narration. كفاه الله همّه من الدنيا could also be read as ‘Allah will suffice him in his concern regarding this world’.</sub><br><sub>p. 347</sub></td>
</tr>
</table>


## From the Counsels of the Messenger of Allah (s.a.w.)

<p dir="rtl" lang="ar"><b>من وصايا رسول الله (صلى الله عليه وآله)</b></p>

<table>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٨٥] وقال رسول الله (صلى الله عليه وآله): طوبى لمن طال عمره وحسن عمله، فحسن منقلبه إذ رضي عنه ربّه، وويل لمن طال عمره وساء عمله، فساء منقلبه إذ أسخط عليه ربّه عزّ وجلّ.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[85] The Messenger of Allah (s.a.w.) said: Blessed is the one whose life is long and whose deeds are good, for his return is good, since his Lord is pleased with him. And woe to the one whose life is long and whose deeds are bad, for his return is bad, since he has brought the anger of his Lord, Mighty and Majestic, upon himself.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> Editor's footnote: the manuscript has إذا for both occurrences of إذ; the editor printed إذ. منقلبه = his final return/destination (in the Hereafter). أسخط عليه ربّه, literally 'he angered his Lord against himself', i.e. he provoked his Lord's displeasure.</sub><br><sub>p. 349</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٨٦] وروى عمرو بن شمر، عن جابر بن يزيد الجعفي، عن أبي جعفر محمّد بن عليّ الباقر (عليه السلام) قال: أوحى الله عزّ وجلّ إلى رسول الله (صلى الله عليه وآله): إنّي شكرت لجعفر بن أبي طالب أربع خصال، فدعاه النبي (صلى الله عليه وآله) فأخبره فقال: لولا إنّه تبارك وتعالى أخبرك ما أخبرتك، ما شربت خمراً قطّ، لأنّي قد علمت أنّي إن شربتها زال عقلي، وما كذّبت قطّ لأنّي علمت أن الكذب ينقص المروّة، وما زنيت قط لأنّي خفت إذا عملت عمل بي، وما عبدت صنماً قطّ لأنّي علمت أنّه لا يضرّ ولا ينفع.<br>قال: فضرب النبيّ (صلى الله عليه وآله) يده على عاتقه، وقال: حقٌّ على الله عزّ وجلّ أن يجعل لك جناحين تطير بهما مع الملائكة في الجنّة.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[86] ʿAmr ibn Shimr narrated from Jābir ibn Yazīd al-Juʿfī, from Abū Jaʿfar Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī al-Bāqir (a.s.), who said: Allah, Mighty and Majestic, revealed to the Messenger of Allah (s.a.w.): "I have appreciated four qualities in Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib." So the Prophet (s.a.w.) called him and told him, and he said: "Had He, Blessed and Exalted, not informed you, I would not have told you. I never drank wine, because I knew that if I drank it my mind would leave me. I never lied, because I knew that lying diminishes one's manliness (muruwwa, honourable character). I never committed fornication, because I feared that if I did it, it would be done to me. And I never worshipped an idol, because I knew that it can neither harm nor benefit."<br>He (al-Bāqir) said: The Prophet (s.a.w.) struck his hand upon his (Jaʿfar's) shoulder and said: "It is binding upon Allah, Mighty and Majestic, to give you two wings with which you will fly with the angels in Paradise."<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> Printed لولا إنّه (hamza clearly below the alif at high zoom); grammatically one expects لولا أنّه; the meaning is unaffected. شكرت لجعفر: literally 'I have thanked/appreciated in Jaʿfar'. عمل بي ('it would be done to me') is usually understood as retaliation falling on one's own household (womenfolk); I kept the literal wording. 'He said' before the Prophet striking his shoulder is taken as the narrator Imam al-Bāqir resuming.</sub><br><sub>p. 349</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٨٧] وقال رسول الله (صلى الله عليه وآله): قال الله جلّ جلاله: عبادي كلكم ضالّ إلاّ من هديته، وكلّكم فقراء إلاّ من أغنيته، وكلّكم مذنب إلاّ من عصمته.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[87] The Messenger of Allah (s.a.w.) said: Allah, exalted be His majesty, said: "My servants, every one of you is astray except the one I have guided, all of you are poor except the one I have made rich, and every one of you is a sinner except the one I have protected (from sin)."<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> Printed فقراء (plural); editor's footnote says al-Faqīh reads فقير (singular), matching ضالّ and مذنب. The first كلكم is printed without shadda, the other two with it. عصمته: 'I have kept him immune/protected' from sin.</sub><br><sub>p. 350</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٨٨] وفي رواية السكوني قال: قال علي (عليه السلام): ما من يوم يمرّ على ابن آدم إلاّ قال له ذلك اليوم: أنا يومٌ جديدٌ وأنا عليك شهيد، فقل فيّ خيراً، واعمل فيّ خيراً، أشهد لك يوم القيامة فإنّك لن تراني بعدها أبداً.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[88] In the narration of al-Sakūnī, he said: ʿAlī (a.s.) said: There is no day that passes over the son of Adam without that day saying to him: "I am a new day, and I am a witness over you. So say good in me and do good in me, and I will testify for you on the Day of Resurrection, for you will never see me again after it."<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> بعدها (feminine pronoun) is literally 'after it', presumably referring to this day or occasion; the editor notes that al-Faqīh reads بعد هذا ('after this'). أشهد is jussive in answer to the imperatives, so it could also be rendered '(if you do so) I will testify for you'.</sub><br><sub>p. 350</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٨٩] وفي رواية مسعدة بن صدقة قال: قال رسول الله (صلى الله عليه وآله): للمؤمن على المؤمن سبعة حقوق واجبة من الله عزّ وجلّ عليه: الإجلال له في عينه، والودّ له في صدره، والمواساة له في ماله، وأن يحرم غيبته، وأن يعوده في مرضه، وأن يشيّع في جنازته، وأن لا يقول بعد موته إلاّ خيراً.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[89] In the narration of Masʿada ibn Ṣadaqa, he said: The Messenger of Allah (s.a.w.) said: A believer has seven rights over a fellow believer, made obligatory upon him by Allah, Mighty and Majestic: to hold him in esteem in his eyes; to have affection for him in his heart; to share his wealth with him; to hold backbiting against him forbidden; to visit him when he is ill; to join the procession at his funeral; and to say nothing but good about him after his death.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> وأن يحرم غيبته: read yaḥrum/yuḥarrim ghībatahu, 'regard backbiting against him as forbidden (inviolable)', i.e. refrain from it. The printed text reads يشيّع في جنازته (with في), which is unusual; يشيّع جنازته, 'escort his bier', is the expected phrasing. المواساة له في ماله: supporting him by sharing one's wealth with him.</sub><br><sub>p. 350</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٩٠] وروى ابن أبي عمير، عن ابن أبي زياد التهمي، عن عبد الله بن وهب، عن الصادق جعفر بن محمّد (عليه السلام) قال: حسب المؤمن من الله نصرة أن يرى عدوّه يعمل بمعاصي الله عزّ وجلّ.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[90] Ibn Abī ʿUmayr narrated from Ibn Abī Ziyād al-Tahmī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wahb, from al-Ṣādiq Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad (a.s.), who said: It is enough help from Allah for the believer that he sees his enemy committing acts of disobedience to Allah, Mighty and Majestic.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The printed name is ابن أبي زياد التهمي; the editor's footnote says al-Faqīh has أبي زياد النهدي (citing Muʿjam rijāl al-ḥadīth 7:320). التهمي is probably a misprint of النهدي. I transliterated the text as printed.</sub><br><sub>p. 350</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٩١] وروى ابن أبي عمير، عن معاوية بن هب، عن الصادق [جعفر بن محمّد] (عليه السلام) قال: اصبر على أعداء النعم، فإنّك لن تكافي من عصي الله فيك بأفضل من أن تطيع الله فيه.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[91] Ibn Abī ʿUmayr narrated from Muʿāwiya ibn [Wa]hb, from al-Ṣādiq [Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad] (a.s.), who said: Be patient with the enemies of (your) blessings, for you will never repay the one who disobeys Allah in regard to you with anything better than obeying Allah in regard to him.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> Printed معاوية بن هب, evidently a misprint for معاوية بن وهب; I transcribed it as printed and flagged the missing letter in the English. The bracketed [جعفر بن محمّد] is the editor's insertion from al-Faqīh. تكافي (for تكافئ) and عصي (for عصى, with a dotted yāʾ) are printed that way. أعداء النعم: those who resent the blessings you enjoy, i.e. the envious.</sub><br><sub>p. 351</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٩٢] وروى المعلّي بن محمّد البصري، عن أحمد بن محمّد بن عبد الله، عن عمرو بن زياد، عن مدرك بن عبد الرحمن، عن أبي عبد الله الصادق [جعفر بن محمّد] (عليه السلام) قال: إذا كان يوم القيامة جمع الله عزّ وجلّ الناس في صعيد واحد ووضعت الموازين، فتوزن دماء الشهداء مع مداد العلماء، فيرجّح مداد العلماء على دماء الشهداء.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[92] al-Muʿallā ibn Muḥammad al-Baṣrī narrated from Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from ʿAmr ibn Ziyād, from Mudrik ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, from Abū ʿAbd Allāh al-Ṣādiq [Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad] (a.s.), who said: When the Day of Resurrection comes, Allah, Mighty and Majestic, will gather the people on a single plain, and the scales will be set up. Then the blood of the martyrs will be weighed against the ink of the scholars, and the ink of the scholars will outweigh the blood of the martyrs.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The name is printed المعلّي (with a dotted yāʾ) for al-Muʿallā. The bracketed [جعفر بن محمّد] is the editor's insertion from al-Faqīh. مع ('with') is rendered 'against' in the sense of weighing one alongside the other. فيرجّح may be read yurajjaḥu ('is made to prevail') or yurajjiḥu; either way it means the ink outweighs the blood.</sub><br><sub>p. 351</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٩٣] وروى محمّد بن أبي عمير، عن عبد الله بن القاسم، عن الصادق جعفر بن محمّد، عن أبيه، عن جدّه، عن علي (عليهم السلام) قال: كن لما لا ترجو أرجى منك لما ترجو، فإنّ موسى بن عمران خرج يقتبس لأهله ناراً فكلّمه الله عزّ وجلّ فرجع نبيّاً، وخرجت ملكة سبأ فأسلمت مع سليمان (عليه السلام)، وخرج سحرة فرعون يطلبون العزّة لفرعون فرجعوا مؤمنين.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[93] Muḥammad ibn Abī ʿUmayr narrated from ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Qāsim, from al-Ṣādiq Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad, from his father, from his grandfather, from ʿAlī (a.s.), who said: Be more hopeful for what you do not hope for than you are for what you do hope for. For Moses son of ʿImrān went out to fetch a firebrand for his family, and Allah, Mighty and Majestic, spoke to him, and he returned a prophet. The Queen of Sheba went out, and she submitted (to Allah) along with Solomon (a.s.). And Pharaoh's sorcerers went out seeking might for Pharaoh, and they returned believers.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The ligature after علي looks like the plural (عليهم السلام), covering the whole chain of Imams; it could be read as singular. These are allusions to Qur'anic passages, not verbatim quotations: Moses and the fire [Q 20:10; 28:29]; the Queen's submission 'with Solomon' echoes [Q 27:44]; the sorcerers seeking Pharaoh's might echoes [Q 26:44], and their becoming believers [Q 7:120-122; 26:46-47]. The opening maxim, literally 'Be, for what you do not hope for, more hopeful than you are for what you hope for', means: expect good from unexpected quarters more than from the ones you count on.</sub><br><sub>p. 351</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٩٤] وروى عبد الله بن عبّاس، عن رسول الله (صلى الله عليه وآله) أنه قال: أشراف أُمّتي حملة القرآن، وأصحاب الليل.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[94] ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās narrated from the Messenger of Allah (s.a.w.) that he said: The nobles of my community are the bearers of the Qur'an and the companions of the night.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> أصحاب الليل ('companions of the night') means those who keep vigil in night prayer. حملة القرآن: those who carry the Qur'an, i.e. its memorizers and practitioners.</sub><br><sub>p. 352</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٩٥] ونزل جبرئيل (عليه السلام) على النبيّ (صلى الله عليه وآله) فقال: يا جبرئيل عظني.<br>فقال: يا محمّد، [ـ أو يا رسول الله ـ] عش ما شئت فإنّك ميّت، وأحبب من شئت فإنّك مفارقه، واعمل ما شئت فإنّك ملاقيه، شرف المؤمن صلواته بالليل، وعزّه كفّ الأذى عن الناس.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[95] Gabriel (a.s.) came down to the Prophet (s.a.w.), and he (the Prophet) said: "O Gabriel, admonish me."<br>He said: "O Muḥammad [or: O Messenger of Allah], live as long as you wish, for you will die. Love whomever you wish, for you will part from him. Do whatever you wish, for you will meet it. The honour of the believer is his prayers at night, and his might is in refraining from harming people."<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> The bracketed [ـ أو يا رسول الله ـ] is in the printed text; the editor's footnote says it appears to be the narrator's hesitation over the wording and that it is absent from al-Faqīh. ملاقيه: 'you will meet it', i.e. encounter the deed and its consequence (in the Hereafter).</sub><br><sub>p. 352</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٩٦] وروى الحسن بن موسى الخشّاب، عن غياث بن كلوب، عن إسحاق بن عمّار، عن الصادق، عن آبائه (عليهم السلام): أنّ عليّاً (عليه السلام) كان يقول: ما من أحد ابتلى وإن عظمت بلواه أحقّ بالدعاء من المعافى الذي لا يؤمن البلاء.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[96] al-Ḥasan ibn Mūsā al-Khashshāb narrated from Ghiyāth ibn Kallūb, from Isḥāq ibn ʿAmmār, from al-Ṣādiq, from his forefathers (a.s.), that ʿAlī (a.s.) used to say: No one who is afflicted, however great his affliction, has more reason to supplicate than the one in good health who is not safe from affliction.<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> ابتلى is printed in the active form; it must be read as the passive ubtuliya ('is afflicted'). لا يؤمن البلاء: 'for whom affliction cannot be ruled out' (lā yuʾmanu [ʿalayhi] al-balāʾ), or 'who is not secure against affliction'. أحقّ بالدعاء: 'more entitled to / more fitting for supplication'. The point is that the healthy person should pray as much as the afflicted one. Printed آبائه; the madda is faint in the scan.</sub><br><sub>p. 352</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٩٧] وروى عليّ بن مهزيار، عن الحسين بن سعيد، عن الحرث بن محمّد بن النعمان الأحول صاحب الطاق، عن جميل بن صالح، عن أبي عبد الله الصادق، عن آبائه (عليهم السلام) قال: قال رسول الله (صلى الله عليه وآله): [من أحبَّ أن يكون أكرم الناس فليتق الله، ومن أحب أن يكون أتقى الناس فليتوكّل على الله،] ومن أحب أن يكون أغنى الناس فليكن بما عند الله عزّ وجلّ أوثق منه بما في يده.<br>ثمّ قال (صلى الله عليه وآله): ألا انبّئكم بشرّ من هذا؟<br>قالوا: بلى يا رسول الله.<br>[قال: من أبغض الناس وأبغضه الناس.<br>ثمّ قال: ألا أنبّئكم بشرٍّ من هذا؟<br>قالوا: بلى يا رسول الله]<br>قال: الذي لا يقبل عثرة، ولا يقبل معذرة، ولا يغفر ذنباً.<br>ثمّ قال: ألا أنبّئكم بشرٍّ من هذا؟<br>قالوا: بلى يا رسول الله.<br>قال: من لا يؤمن شرّه ولا يرجى خيره، إنّ عيسى بن مريم (عليه السلام) مرّ في بني إسرائيل فقال: يا بني إسرائيل، لا تحدّثوا بالحكمة الجهّال فتظلموها، ولا تمنعوها أهلها فتظلموهم، ولا تعينوا الظالم على ظلمه فيبطل فضلكم.<br>الأمور ثلاثة: أمر بيّن لك رشده فاتّبعه، وأمر بين لك غيّه فاجتنبه، وأمر اختلف فيه فردّه إلى الله عزّ وجلّ.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[97] ʿAlī ibn Mahziyār narrated from al-Ḥusayn ibn Saʿīd, from al-Ḥārith ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Nuʿmān al-Aḥwal Ṣāḥib al-Ṭāq, from Jamīl ibn Ṣāliḥ, from Abū ʿAbd Allāh al-Ṣādiq, from his forefathers (a.s.), who said: The Messenger of Allah (s.a.w.) said: [Whoever would like to be the most noble of people, let him be mindful of Allah (taqwa, God-consciousness); and whoever would like to be the most God-conscious of people, let him rely on Allah;] and whoever would like to be the richest of people, let him be more confident in what is with Allah, Mighty and Majestic, than in what is in his own hand.<br>Then he (s.a.w.) said: "Shall I tell you of one who is worse than this?"<br>They said: "Yes, O Messenger of Allah."<br>[He said: "One who hates people and whom people hate."<br>Then he said: "Shall I tell you of one who is worse than this?"<br>They said: "Yes, O Messenger of Allah."]<br>He said: "The one who does not overlook a slip, does not accept an excuse, and does not forgive a sin."<br>Then he said: "Shall I tell you of one who is worse than this?"<br>They said: "Yes, O Messenger of Allah."<br>He said: "The one whose evil is not safe from and whose good is not hoped for. Jesus son of Mary (a.s.) passed among the Children of Israel and said: 'O Children of Israel, do not speak wisdom to the ignorant, lest you wrong it; do not withhold it from those worthy of it, lest you wrong them; and do not help the oppressor in his oppression, lest your merit be nullified.'"<br>"Matters are of three kinds: a matter whose right course is clear to you, so follow it; a matter whose error is clear to you, so avoid it; and a matter over which there is disagreement, so refer it back to Allah, Mighty and Majestic."<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> This narration spans pp. 352-353 and ends on p. 353 with the editor's source footnote, so the chunk ends there. Both bracketed passages (on 352 and 353) are the editor's insertions from al-Faqīh; the editor also notes al-Faqīh reads قام ('stood') for مرّ ('passed') and تبيّن for the first بيّن. The first question is printed انبّئكم (no hamza) بشرّ, the later ones أنبّئكم بشرٍّ. Without the bracketed insertion, the first 'worse than this' oddly follows positive advice, which suggests the base text had a lacuna. Printed الحرث is the defective spelling of al-Ḥārith. It is ambiguous whether 'Matters are of three kinds...' continues Jesus's words or is the Prophet resuming; I left it as a separate quoted paragraph without assigning it. لا يقبل عثرة: 'does not pardon/overlook a stumble'. فتظلموها: 'you wrong it (wisdom)' by placing it where it does not belong.</sub><br><sub>p. 352</sub></td>
</tr>
</table>


## From the Counsels of Imam al-Ṣādiq (a.s.)

<p dir="rtl" lang="ar"><b>من وصايا الإمام الصادق (عليه السلام)</b></p>

<table>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٩٨] روى الحسن بن عليّ بن فضّال، عن الحسن بن الجهم، عن الفضيل بن يسار، قال: قال الصادق جعفر بن محمّد (عليهما السلام): ما ضعف بدن عمّا قويت عليه النيّة.</td>
<td width="50%" valign="top">[98] Al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ibn Faḍḍāl narrated from al-Ḥasan ibn al-Jahm, from al-Fuḍayl ibn Yasār, who said: al-Ṣādiq Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad (a.s.) said: 'A body never falls short of what the intention has grown strong enough for.'<br><br><sub><b>Notes:</b> Literally: 'A body has not weakened from that for which the intention has become strong.' The sense is that firm resolve carries the body through. The honorific ligature after 'Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad' here and in 99, 105, 106, 107 and 112 appears to be the dual form (عليهما السلام), as is usual after a name of the form 'X ibn Y'. The ligatures are small in the scan.</sub><br><sub>p. 354</sub></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td dir="rtl" lang="ar" width="50%" valign="top">[٩٩] وروى الحسن بن فضّال، عن غالب بن عثمان، عن شعيب العقرقوفي، عن الصادق جعفر بن محمّد (عليهما السلام) قال: مَن ملك نفسه إذا رغب وإذا رهب، وإذا اشتهى وإذا غضب، فإذا رضي حرّم الله جسده على النار.</td>
